15. Mocking Epithets
οἱ περὶ
Πυθαγόραν οἱ Ληκυθισμοί
να eT followers of Lekythismoí action of
Pythagoras a lekythos
(c. 530 BCE onward)

30

On Piety 806 & 1398

Origen Contr. Cels. 7.66

A lekythos is a clay oil flask — an empty one makes a hollow, clattering noise.

Pythagorean teachings were steeped in secret symbols

and the esoteric jargon of mystical numerology.

The adherents of Pythagoras spoke with words empty of meaning:

both the flask and the students make noise but have no actual content.

Ἡράκλειτος
l Heraclitus .. ὁ Κυκητής
the Agitator eee Kyketés ..
(fl. 500 gce)

Heraclitus was fundamentally a skeptic, saying:

"let us not hazard guesses about the most important matters" (Laertius 9.73)

Plato mocked the Heracliteans and their theory of fiery flux by calling them

οἱ ῥέοντες "the fluxing ones" (theaetetus 180c)

"The Stoics together with Heraclitus say that matter is wholly and completely changeable

and alterable and mutable and fluid." (aétius 1.9.2)

Epicurus escalates the irony: not just calling Heraclitus a "Fluxor" — but an "Agitator."

Epicurus' term alludes to Heraclitus' own metaphor of

the kykeon (a mixed barley drink) which will separate if not stirred (pk 8125)

Πρωταγόρας sO "m
ὁ Φορμοφόρο
the Basketbearer Protagoras Phormophóros d a
wicker basket carrying
(fl. 450 &CE)

According to Athenaeus:
"in the same letter [On Occupations], Epicurus also says that Protagoras, the sophist,
from first being a basket-carrier and firewood-carrier, he later became Democritus' scribe
(with Protagoras having been admired by Democritus for some

particular invention for carrying firewood)" (Deipnosophists, 8.50, 354C)

According to Laertius:

"Protagoras was the first to invent the so-called 'Tyle' (on which people carry burdens)" (9.53)

οἱ περὶ
Πρωταγόραν οἱ Βαρυεγκέφαλοι
the Thickskulls > ο Baryengképhaloi ας
followers of heavy head
Protagoras

Protagoras was a student of Democritus — even though Democritus was about 30 years younger.

Epicurus highlights Protagoras' humble origins as a manual laborer, his late start in philosophy, and

his misapplication of Democritus' science in founding the "profession" of sophistry.

οἱ περὶ
Σωκράτη όν "
- αἱ Ἀλαζονεῖαι
the Pretenders followers of Alazonefai
vagrant / boaster
Socrates

(430 BCE onward)

Socrates took the skepticism that was rampant in physical science and applied it to human life.

The term "Pretenders" applied to followers of Socrates as a jab at their

public posturing and affected ignorance.
For example Phaedo of Elis, a close student, and the main speaker in Plato's "Phaedo."
Phaedo later returned to his hometown and founded the Elean School of philosophy (teaching that

virtue is knowledge, the mind is the sole source of the good, and the soul is immortal).

Cicero reports that Epicurus "foully slandered Phaedo [of Elis], the disciple of Socrates" (ono 1.93)

. Δημόκριτος . ,
the Arbiter of de a E ὁ Ληρόκριτος
Es Democritus Lerókritos
Absurdities judge of nonsense
(fl. 420 BCE)

31

Democritus was an eliminative materialist (believing that only the atoms and the void really exist)
and extreme skeptic, saying: "men know nothing, for truth is in an abyss" (Laertius 9.72)
and he denied that any criterion of knowledge exists, holding that

"none of the apparent things is a criterion" (106)

Democritus thought that the gods deliberately communicate with men via the films,

and he thought the films have their own perception.

Democritus also took a particular interest in zombies.
According to Proclus (Commentary on the Republic, 2.113.6)
"Stories of people who appeared to have died and then came back to life

were collected by many of the ancients including the scientist Democritus in his writings..."

ὁ Χρυσοῦ
the Golden One Πλάτων Ἀριστοκλῆς Chrysoüs P ?
: shining
Plato Aristocles
(fl. c 388 Bce)

Born at the end of the golden age — during the great plague of Athens — Plato eventually "created a

synthesis of the doctrines of Heraclitus, Pythagoras, and Socrates." (Laertius 3.8)

Plato is fundamentally a Skeptic, leaving truth "to the gods"

and only hoping for "probable explanations." (Laertius 9.72, Timaeus 29c)

οἱ περὶ
Ἡρακλείδην = :
- αἱ Ἀνδροφονίαι

Heracleides
of Aenus

A pupil of Plato, Heracleides of Aenus took part in the assassination

of the King Cotys I of Thrace around 360 BCE

οἱ περὶ
the Dionysus- Πλάτωνα ] οἱ Διονυσοκόλακες
Dionysokólakes
Flatterers followers of Dionysus flatterer
Plato

A reference to King Dionysius, the tyrant of Syracuse.
Epicurus is ridiculing the Academy's sycophantic pursuit of political influence:
especially Plato's own ill-fated attempt to tutor King Dionysius Il —

who ultimately detained Plato against his will.

oi Κυζικηνοί οἱ Ἐχθροὶ
the Enemies of i i n Echthroi 8 i 4
the Cyzicenes . f τῆς Ἑλλάδος
Greece tés Hellados
(350 BCE onward) hating

the Cyzicenes here refers to the students of Eudoxus of Cnidos (Κνίδος), the mathematical
astronomer and associate of Plato. Eudoxus taught at Cyzicus (Κύζικος) and set up a school there.
(Cyzicus is on the Sea of Marmara about 35 miles east of Lampsacus.)
Eudoxus' teachings on pleasure were similar to Epicurus.
Eudoxus died right around when Epicurus was born, but he left many influential pupils in Cyzicus,

who also spread their influence from there to Athens.

Ἀριστοτέλης τ
i - ὁ Ἄσωτος
the Degenerate Aristotle Asotos
not saving
(fl. 344 BCE)

Aristotle wasted his inheritance and then turned to military service and selling drugs —
presumably in connection with his peddling of remedies, we also hear of Aristotle:

"bathing in a bath of warm oil and then selling the oil." (Laertius 5.16)

- : oi Πολύφθοροι
οἱ Διαλεκτικοί ; ,
the Grand Wreckers . ND Polyphthoroi having damaged
Dialecticians h
muc

Epicurus grouped together the Aristotelians, Academic Skeptics, Megarian logicians, and Stoics

as purveyors of fruitless debate that could be destructive to living well.

By dubbing all dialecticians "the Grand Wreckers," Epicurus highlighted the deep rift
between his philosophy and the logic-oriented approach
of the builders of false systems such as the Aristotelians

and of the outright destructive skepticism of the Megarians such as Diodorus Cronus.

Θεόφραστον :

heavy tone

Theophrastus *

32

* This application to Theophrastus only is by process of elimination.
barystonoi was colloquial slang for actors who utter deep tragic groans.
possibly parodying Theophrastean style as long-winded,

emotionally burdensome, or affectedly serious.

Ἀντίδωρος ὁ Σαννίδωρος
the Wood-Giver Antidorus Sannídoros gift of
(fl.c 330 BCE) penis / plank

We know that Epicurus wrote a two book series on Antidorus.

(Possibly Antidorus of Cyme the grammarian)

αἱ Βωμολοχίαι

ambushing
the Altar-Beggars cine! Bomolochiai fe. beronani
Ἱππαρχίαν
Px the altar
followers of
Hipparchia
of Maroneia αἱ Ἑταιρήσεις
the Courtesanry Hetairéseis process of being
(fl.c. 325 BCE)

a courtesan

Hipparchia (whose name means "ruler of horses") was the wife of Crates of Thebes
and a student of Diogenes of Sinope.
Calling the students of Diogenes "altar-beggars" was likely literally true,
and calling his female adherents "the courtesanry" was humorous

because it ironically elevated their status.

: ὁ Ἀμαθή
stupid , Amatheés paid
Πύρρων not learning
Pyrrho
(fl. 320 BCE) ] ὁ Ἀπαίδευτος
uneducated Apaideutos

not educated

Pyrrho was an extreme relativist, saying "for each assertion, there is an opposing assertion" (9.74)

and "each thing is no more this, then that."

He was also an extreme skeptic, Pyrrho and his followers "themselves determined nothing, not even
[that they determined nothing]. and consequently they even rejected not determining" (9.74) while

holding that "the senses deceive, and reason is inconsistent." (9.95)

Pyrrho and his followers employed logical puzzles in
"rejecting all demonstration, criterion, sign, cause, motion, learning, coming into being —

and the idea that anything is good or bad by nature." (9.90)

His school maintained a famous list of 10 ways (τρόποι)

in which sensation and certainty are undermined.

Despite all this, Pyrrho also served as a high priest at the Temple of Zeus at Olympia.

Jellyfish : oi Πλεύμονες
Pleumones
literally, "(sea) lungs" sides / lungs
- ; Ἀγράμματος
illiterate Ναυσιφάνης Agrammatos
not the result of writing
Nausiphanes v :
Ρ i ὁ Άπατεων
swindler (fl.c. 310 BCE) Apateon
deceiving agent
"me ἡ Πόρν
prostitute Pórne ΠΠ
selling

Epicurus used the term "Jellyfish" to specifically indicate insensitivity and senselessness.

(Sextus Empiricus, Against the Grammarians, 1.4)

Nausiphanes was a student of Pyrrho. In support of skepticism, Nausiphanes is known to have said:

"Of those things which appear to exist, nothing exists more than it does not exist."

Nausiphanes took some of his science from Democritus (through texts or tradition). In support of
his 'science-based political rhetoric,’ Philodemus quotes Nausiphanes as saying, "honor depends on
winning a reputation for cleverness in politics... the wise man is he who can persuade his hearers —

and this power of persuasion belongs to the man of science."

Human Weakness

Philodemus (µε. 70 8), On The Gods, Book 3, P.Herc. 152, fr. 9/57

«χρειωδῶν ἡ συμφυλία π/ρὸς τὴν] συνανα/σ]τροφὴν αὐτῆι τὰ πάθη παρ[αδί]δωσιν. οὐ γὰρ δυνατὸν ἔχειν τὴν συμφυλίαν
ἄνε[υ] πάσης ἐπιμειξίας ὄντας. ἀμέλει δὲ klai) ἐφ' ἡμῶν τῶν ἀσθενῶν klal) πρίοσ)δεγομένων πρ[ὸς τ]ὰ [χρ]ειώδη τ[ῆς]

Φιλίας [οὐκ ἔτι φτω τὸ λοι[πὸν ὑ]ποθλ[ jol [τας]...

..χρειωδῶν ἡ Συμφυλία πρὸς τὴν συναναστροφὴν αὐτῇ τὰ πάθη παραδίδωσιν:
οὐ γὰρ δυνατὸν Ἔχειν τὴν συμφυλίαν ἄνευ πάσης ἐπιμειξίας, ὄντας

..the similarity [arising] from necessities, in regards to association, gives the
feelings over to [that similarly]: for maintaining similarity, while existing
[together], is not possible without any mixture [of feelings]

ἀμέλει δὲ, καὶ ἐφ' ἡμῶν, τῶν ἀσθενῶν καὶ προσδεομένων πρὸς τὰ χρειώδη τῆς
φιλίας...

indeed, certainly for us, who are weak and have further need for the
necessary things of friendship...

Regarding imperfection, Philodemus directly connects human weakness with our
need for friendship. While discussing the importance of mutual friendship (ἡ
συμφυλία) he says that generosity is necessary.

Hó-ros toi me-gé-thous tôn he-do-nón hē pan-tos τοῦ al-goün-tos Hy-pe-xai-re-sis

hó-pou d'an To he-dó-me-non e-né — kath' hon an chró-non ê: ouk és-ti To al-goün ὅ

λυπούμενον ~ ἢ Τὸ συναμφότερον

wherever What pleases is present — during that time it exists: there is
neither What pains or distresses — or What does both

36

Pain and pleasure are mutually exclusive at any particular point in the body.

Epicurus is, in part, responding to the teachings of the Cyrenaics, who viewed the
removal of pain as a state of calm to which pleasure could then be added.

Epicurus is also responding to Plato who argued for the existence of mixed pleasures
(µικταί ἡδοναί) — which Plato imagined contained an aspect of pain.

"The Good" is Escaping "The Bad"

Metrodorus, quoted by Plutarch (p. 80 *), Non Posse 1091 A

Τοῦτο αὐτὸ τὸ ἀγαθόν ἐστι: τὸ Φυγεῖν τὸ κακόν — ἔνθα yàp τεθήσεται τἀγαθόν
οὐκ ἔστιν, ὅταν Μηθὲν ἔτι ὑπεξίῃ μήτε ἀλγεινὸν μήτε λυπηρόν

This very thing is the good: Escaping the bad — because it is not possible for
the good to be placed anywhere, when Nothing painful or distressing is
further withdrawing

37

It is common for people to consider the elimination of pain as the onset of pleasure.
In fact, the absence of pain, and the resulting state of painlessness, is precisely what
constitutes pleasure.

Full physical contentment is consistently and naturally achieved through our body's
internal processes when we have the necessary provisions of food and shelter.

Similarly, full mental contentment can be maintained by recognizing the ease with
which physical contentment can be obtained and by cultivating gratitude for this
SUCCESS.

KA 4. The Limit of Pain

ou chronízei To algoün synechós en té sarki: alla to men Akron ton eláchiston

heméras symbainei

οὐ χρονίζει Τὸ ἀλγοῦν συνεχῶς £v τῇ σαρκί: ἀλλὰ τὸ μὲν Ἄκρον τὸν ἐλάχιστον
χρόνον πάρεστι — Τὸ δὲ µόνον ὑπερτεῖνον τὸ ἡδόμενον κατὰ σάρκα οὐ πολλὰς
ἡμέρας συμβαίνει

What causes pain does not linger continuously in the flesh: but the highest
Point is present for the most minuscule time — and That which merely
exceeds what pleases in relation to the body does not endure for many days

hai de Polychrónioi tôn arrostión pleonázon échousi to hedómenon en té sarki

éper to algoün

αἱ δὲ Πολυχρόνιοι τῶν ἀρρωστιῶν πλεονάζον ἔχουσι τὸ ἡδόμενον ἐν τῇ σαρκὶ
ἤπερ τὸ ἀλγοῦν

and the Long-lasting symptoms of illnesses have what pleases exceeding in
the body over the painful

The significance of pain in life is often overestimated — in fact our body's sensory
areas generally experience natural pleasure when free from disturbance.

Pain and pleasure are mutually exclusive at any specific point in the body —
nevertheless, of course, some parts of the body may experience pain while other
parts of the body do not feel pain.

KA 9. Variance of Pleasures

ei ka-te-pyk-noü-to pá-sa He-do-ne kai chró-no kai pe-ri hó-lon to áth-rois-ma hy-pér-chen

(8 ta ky-ri-6-ta-ta mé-ré tés phy-se-ds) — ouk án po-te di-é-phe-ron al-lé-lon hai Hé-do-nai

εἰ κατεπυκνοῦτο πᾶσα Ἡδονὴ καὶ χρόνῳ καὶ περὶ ὅλον τὸ ἄθροισμα ὑπῆρχεν
(ἢ τὰ κυριώτατα µέρη τῆς φύσεως) - οὐκ ἄν ποτε διέφερον ἀλλήλων αἱ Ἡδοναί

if every Pleasure was fully concentrated both in time and existed in the
whole organism (or in the most essential parts of its nature) -- then
Pleasures would never differ from one another

A counterfactual argument.

This statement is in part a response to the view of the Cyrenaics — a general
grouping of philosophers following Aristippos ("the best-horse") of Kyréné (an
ancient city in what is now Libya). Aristippos, who himself was originally a follower
of Socrates, thought that:

(1) pleasures do not differ from one another
(2) one pleasure is not more or less pleasant than another, and
(3) any particular pleasure is momentary, unable to be prolonged

This incorrect understanding leads to indiscrimination in choosing pleasures.

In reality, even though pleasure cannot be increased beyond the absence of pain,
pleasures are variable in duration (from momentary to continuous) and location
(affecting different parts of the body, including the mind) and have different
qualities. Therefore, discrimination is required in choosing pleasures.

Another counterfactual argument. Epicurus implies that degenerate levels of
pleasure are blameworthy on a practical level because they do not remove fear and
do not teach the natural limits of desire — but instead lead to pain or distress.

while not understanding what the nature of the entirety is (but instead
suspected some of those things according to the myths) — therefore it is not
possible, without natural science, to fully acquire undiluted pleasures

Epicurus emphasizes that a fundamental understanding of how the universe
operates is essential to avoid living in uncertainty and fear.

It is in ΠΟ way a benefit to provide for oneself stability in regards to people

when the things above have been established as suspicions — and the things

under the earth — and the things absolutely in the infinite

We get nothing by getting protection from others if we still fear the general

operations of the universe.

The things above (τὰ ἄνωθεν) and the things under the earth (τὰ ὑπὸ γῆς) are all

things in our cosmos (κόσμος) that are difficult for us to closely observe and study —

whereas the things in the infinite (τὰ ἐν τῷ ἀπείρῳ) are the things in other cosmoi

(kócpoy and in the spaces between.

KA 18. Practical Satisfaction

ouk a-paü-xe-ti en té sar-ki hē Hé-do-né e-pei-dan há-pax to ka-t'én-dei-an Al-goün

e-xai-re-thé — al-la mó-non poi-kíl-le-tai

οὐκ ἀπαύξετι ἐν τῇ σαρκὶ ἡ Ἡδονή ἐπειδὰν ἅπαξ τὸ κατ’ ἔνδειαν Ἀλγοῦν
ἐξαιρεθῇ - ἀλλὰ μόνον ποικίλλεται

Pleasure is not increased further in the flesh as soon as what Hurts due to
deficiency is removed - but it only diversifies

tés de di-a-noí-as to pé-ras to ka-ta tén hé-do-nén a-pe-gén-né-sen hé te (toü-tón au-tdn)

Ek-ló-gi-sis (kai ton ho-mo-ge-nón toü-tois — Hó-sa tous me-gís-tous phó-bous

pa-res-keü-a-ze té di-a-noi-a)

τῆς δὲ διανοίας τὸ πέρας τὸ κατὰ τὴν ἡδονὴν ἀπεγέννησεν ἥ τε (τούτων αὐτῶν)
Ἐκλόγισις (καὶ τῶν ὁμογενῶν τούτοις - Ὅσα τοὺς μεγίστους φόβους
παρεσκεύαζε τῇ διανοίᾳ)

and rational Consideration (of these things and things of the same kind as
those — as many things as used to arrange the greatest fears for our mental
perception) fully produces the mind's limit in regards to pleasure

The point where the amount of pleasure is at its maximum in the body is the painless

state reached by removing the pains of natural and necessary needs. Wealth can
only vary the means by which we can achieve the very same natural pleasure that a
non-wealthy life achieves.

It a common misconception to think that wealth and luxury increase pleasure.
Wealth does not increase pleasure. Attempts to increase pleasure beyond this
maximum point will always fail, and will often involve the introduction of
unnecessary pain and troubles.

KA 19. Fulfillment of Time

ho á-pei-ros Chró-nos í-sen é-chei ten hé-do-nén kai ho pe-pe-ras-mé-nos — e-án Tis au-tés ta

πέρατα καταμετρήσῃ τῷ λογισμῷ

infinite and finite time have equal pleasure — if one fully measures its limits
by reasoning

Because no pleasure is greater than the absence of bodily and mental pain, complete
pleasure is attained by our natural state when not in pain.

More time cannot produce a greater pleasure than this complete pleasure.

the flesh fully acquires the limit of pleasure as infinite, and the Time as

he de Di-á-noi-a, toü tés sar-kos té-lous kai pé-ra-tos la-boü-sa ton e-pi-lo-gis-mon

pa-re-skeü-a-se: kai ou-then é-ti τοῦ α-ρεί-γοι chró-nou pro-se-de-é-thé

ἡ δὲ Διάνοια, τοῦ τῆς σαρκὸς τέλους καὶ πέρατος λαβοῦσα τὸν ἐπιλογισμὸν
— καὶ τοὺς ὑπὲρ τοῦ αἰῶνος φόβους ἐκλύσασα --τὸν παντελῆ βίον
παρεσκεύασε: καὶ οὐθὲν ἔτι τοῦ ἀπείρου χρόνου προσεδεήθη

but mental Perception, grasping the inductive consideration of the
fulfillment and limit of the flesh — and dispelling fears about eternity —
arranges the complete life: and in no way is it still also in need of infinite

time

all' oü-te é-phy-ge ten hé-do-nén — ou-d'hé-ni-ka ten e-xa-gd-gén ek τοῦ zén ta Prág-ma-ta

pa-re-skeü-a-zen hos el-lei-pou-sa ti τοῦ a-rís-tou bi-ou ka-té-strep-sen

ἀλλ’ οὔτε ἔφυγε τὴν ἡδονὴν - οὐδ’ (ἡνίκα τὴν ἐξαγωγὴν ἐκ τοῦ ζῆν τὰ Πράγματα
παρεσκεύαζεν) ὡς ἐλλείπουσά τι τοῦ ἀρίστου βίου κατέστρεψεν

yet [our mental perception] does not avoid pleasure -- nor (when Situations
provide the departure from living) does it overturn as though it were leaving
behind something of the best life

The complete pleasure of being pain free has been frequently achieved throughout
our lives, and having a longer life does not allow us to increase the complete
pleasure that we have already experienced.

Understanding this, we are pleased to continue living our full and complete life. But
because our life is full and complete, we understand that continuing to live cannot

improve our lives.

ὁ τὰ πέρατα τοῦ βίου Κατειδὼς οἶδεν ὡς εὐπόριστόν ἐστι τὸ ἀλγοῦν
κατ’ ἔνδειαν Ἐξαιροῦν καὶ τὸ τὸν ὅλον βίον παντελῆ Καθιστάν - ὥστε οὐδὲν

He who understands the limits of life knows how well acquirable is that
which Removes what causes pain due to lack and that which Establishes the
entire life as absolutely complete — so that in no way does he also have need

of situations involving conflicts

As long as we are not in an environment hostile to human life, we do not need to
engage in competitive struggles against others in order gain all our natural needs.

to hy-phes-té-kos Def té-los e-pi-lo-gí-zes-thai kai pà-san ten e-nár-gei-an (e-ph'hén ta

do-xa-zó-me-na A-ná-go-men) - ei de mē, Pán-ta ak-ri-si-as kai ta-ra-chés és-tai mes-tá

τὸ ὑφεστηκὸς Asi τέλος ἐπιλογίζεσθαι καὶ πᾶσαν τὴν ἐνάργειαν (ἐφ’ ἣν τὰ
δοξαζόμενα Ἀνάγομεν) - εἰ δὲ μὴ, Πάντα ἀκρισίας καὶ ταραχῆς ἔσται μεστά

the established fulfilment is necessary to inductively consider as well as all

detectible reality (to which we refer the things that are judged) - otherwise,
Everything will be full of indistinction and disturbance

Epicurus transitions from ethics to epistemology in this κύρια δόξα.

Unless our conclusions (xà δοξαζόμενα) are tested in reference to external

sensations (αἰσθήσεις) and internal sensations (τὰ πάθη) we will go about our lives

with added opinions (προσδοξαζόμενα) that do not correspond to reality.

KA 23. Doubting All Sensations

ei Ma-ché pá-sais tais ais-thé-se-sin: oukh Hé-xeis ou-d'has an Phés au-tón di-e-pseüs-thai

pros tí poi-ou-me-nos ten a-gd-gén Kri-nés

εἰ Μαχῇ πάσαις ταῖς αἰσθήσεσιν: οὐχ Ἔξεις οὐδ’ (ἃς ἂν Φῇς αὐτῶν διεψεῦσθαι)
πρὸς τί ποιούμενος τὴν ἀγωγὴν Κρίνῃς

if You contend against all sensations: then You not will have anything (which
You could even assert has thoroughly given a false report about themselves)
You could judge while bringing up a reference to something

To address the extreme position of some skeptics that all sensations are
untrustworthy, Epicurus argues that if someone rejects all sensations, there is no
other standard of judgment to rely on.

Anyone who claims to use logic as a standard for judgment fails to recognize that
the relationship between their logic and reality is inherently based on their own
observations.

also, if You affirm l"! whatis still pending [attestation] all together in internal

[still pending] M! attestation: then You will not throw out what thoroughly
gives a false report — since You will have preserved all mutual opposition
regarding all distinction of what is right or not right

If you do not distinguish between your ideas and your sensations and you reject even
one sensation as false, you are rejecting the criteria that you have to understand the
world.

Sensations are true per se, but ideas are only true if they accurately reflect our
repeated sensations.

The fact that the tower appears to have rounded edges when viewed from various
distances is accurately demonstrated by the senses.

The sensation of a round tower is a real sensation. However, after seeing the tower
more closely and from other angles, the issue of its shape changes from what is
pending confirmation (τὸ προσμένον τὴν ἐπιμαρτύρησιν) to what is not pending
confirmation (τὸ μὴ προσμένον τὴν ἐπιμαρτύρησιν).

ἡ πάσα φανταστική the whole appearance-based
ἐπιβολή τῆς διανοίας attention of mental perception

Because all true thought is based upon impressions from
external sources, the use of "appearance-based
(davtaotuKkn)" here is only further explaining the object
(i.e., basis) of mental focus.

The whole phrase is therefore equivalent to the attention
of mental perception (ἡ ἐπιβολή τῆς διανοίας)” alone,
which Lucretius translates (2.740) as "a casting out of the
mind (animi iniectus). "

The paraphrases animi iniectus and ἡ επιβολή τῆς
διανοίας therefore are equivalent to our modern use of

"attention" or "focus."

εἰ μὴ παρὰ πάντα καιρὸν Ἐπανοίσεις ἕκαστον τῶν πραττομένων ἐπὶ τὸ τέλος τῆς
φύσεως - ἀλλὰ Προκαταστρέφψεις (εἴτε ᾠυγὴν εἴτε δίωξιν ποιούμενος) εἰς ἄλλο
τι: οὐκ ἔσονταί σοι τοῖς λόγοις αἱ Πράξεις ἀκόλουθοι

if You will not on every opportunity refer each of the things being
accomplished back again to the fulfillment of your nature - but beforehand
you overturn (when making an avoidance or pursuit) into something else:
then your Activities will not be consistent with your reasonings

If you stop short and turn to some other fulfillment (τέλος) before considering the
fulfillment of nature (τὸ τέλος τῆς φύσεως), then the natural goal of mental
tranquillity (ἡ ἡσυχία) is forgotten and is replaced by some other goal that is either
natural but not necessary (φυσικαὶ καὶ οὐκ ἀναγκαῖαι) or neither natural or
necessary (οὔτε φυσικαὶ οὔτ᾽ ἀναγκαῖαι).

Cicero (n. 66 8“), De Finibus, 1.68

"eadem," inquit, "Scientia confirmavit animum ne quod aut sempiternum aut
diütürnüm timeret malum — quae perspexit in hoc ipso vitae spatio amicitiae
praesidium esse firmissimum"

"[it is] the same Knowledge" [Epicurus] says "that strengthened the mind so
that it did not fear an eternal or prolonged evil — which thoroughly saw that
within this very span of life, the support of friendship is the strongest"

ἐν αὐτοῖς τοῖς ὡρισμένοις "in those limited things themselves" — referring to what is
terrible (τὸ δεινὸν) which is limited by being in no way perpetual (μηθὲν αἰώνιον)
and in no way long-lasting (μηδὲ πολυχρόνιον) — and therefore meaning "in the
midst of life's limited evils."

In contrast to this interpretation, Cicero understood it as a reference to life in
general (which is limited by death) — "within this very span of life (in hoc ipso vitae
spatio)"

KA 30. Natural & Unnecessary Desires

en hais tôn phy-si-kón e-pi-thy-mi-ón mē ep'al-goün de e-pa-na-gou-són e-an mē
syn-te-les-thó-sin — hy-pár-chei hē Spou-de syn-to-nos: pa-ra ke-nen dó-xan Hat-tai gi-non-tai
= kai ou para tén he-au-tón phy-sin ou di-a-ché-on-tai, al-la pa-ra ἴδη tod an-thró-pou

ke-no-do-xí-an

ἐν αἷς τῶν φυσικῶν ἐπιθυμιῶν μὴ ἐπ’ ἀλγοῦν δὲ ἐπαναγουσῶν ἐὰν μὴ
συντελεσθῶσιν — ὑπάρχει ἡ Σπουδὴ σύντονος: παρὰ κενὴν δόξαν Αὗται γίνονται
- καὶ οὐ παρὰ τὴν ἑαυτῶν φύσιν οὐ διαχέονται, ἀλλὰ παρὰ τὴν τοῦ ἀνθρώπου
κενοδοξίαν

in those natural desires not leading back to feeling pain if not altogether
fulfilled — when intense Eagerness exists: These [desires] are produced from
empty judgement - and not because of their own nature that they are not
scattered, but because empty judgment of man

οὐ παρὰ τὴν ἑαυτῶν φύσιν "not because of their own nature" — when we develop
intense desires for what not necessary (οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον) and also not easy to get
(οὐκ εὐπόριστον/) — we have a problem that arises completely from our thoughts
and not from any actual need.

In KA 26, Epicurus pointed out that all desires that do not bring pain when unfulfilled,
are unnecessary.

In KA 29, he distinguished between desires that are natural and not necessary
(φυσικαὶ καὶ οὐκ ἀναγκαῖαι) versus those that are neither not natural and not
necessary (οὔτε φυσικαὶ οὔτ᾽ ἀναγκαῖαι).

Now he reminds us that it is foolish to pursue even natural desires if they are not
necessary and also difficult to attain.

KA 33. Practical Justice

ouk ên ti ka-th'he-au-to Di-kai-o-sy-né — al-l' en tais me-t'al-lé-lon sys-tro-phais kath'

ho-pé-li-kous: dé-po-te a-ei tó-pous Syn-thé-ké tis hy-per tod mē bláp-tein é bláp-tes-thai

οὐκ ἦν τι καθ’ ἑαυτὸ Δικαιοσύνη -- ἀλλ’ ἐν ταῖς uev ἀλλήλων συστροφαῖς καθ’
ὁπηλίκους: δήποτε ἀεὶ τόπους Συνθήκη τις ὑπὲρ τοῦ μὴ βλάπτειν ἢ βλάπτεσθαι

Justice is not something in itself -- but instead in the assemblages with each
other of any magnitude: in whatever locations there is always some mutual
Agreement regarding not harming or being harmed

Justice does not exist per se and is not metaphysical or universal. Rather, it exists
only among those who are both able and willing to establish and uphold agreements

that serve mutual interests.

KA 35. Consequence of Secrecy

ouk És-ti ton lá-thré ti Poi-oün-ta (hôn Sy-né-then-to pros al-lé-lous eis to me

bláp-tein mé-de bláp-tes-thai) pis-teü-ein hó-ti Lé-sei — kan my-ri-á-kis e-pi toü

pa-rón-tos La-thá-n& — mé-chri gar ka-tas-tro-phés, á-dé-lon ei kai Lé-sei

οὐκ Ἔστι τὸν λάθρῃ τι Ποιοῦντα (ὧν Συνέθεντο πρὸς ἀλλήλους εἰς τὸ μὴ
βλάπτειν μηδὲ βλάπτεσθαι) πιστεύειν ὅτι Λήσει — κἄν μυριάκις ἐπὶ τοῦ
παρόντος Λαθάνῃ - μέχρι γὰρ καταστροφῆς, ἄδηλον εἰ καὶ Λήσει

It is not possible for one who evasively brings about something (among
those things that they mutually agreed with one another in not harming and
not being harmed) to believe that he will evade notice — even if he evades
thousands of times up to the present — for until his overturning, it is unclear
if he will still evade notice

69

It is impossible for someone who secretly breaks an agreement to be confident they
will avoid detection. This state of anxiety persists until death or exposure — so the
moral and psychological consequences of breaking such a compact are significant.

koi-no-ní-as (tôn no-mis-thén-ton εἴ-παἰ di-kaí-on) é-chei τοῦ di-kaí-ou cha-rak-té-ra — e-án te

to au-to pa-si gé-né-tai e-án te me to au-tó

τὸ μὲν Ἐπιμαρτυρούμενον ὅτι συμφέρει ἐν ταῖς χρείαις τῆς πρὸς ἀλλήλους
κοινωνίας (τῶν νομισθέντων εἶναι δικαίων) ἔχει τοῦ δικαίου χαρακτῆρα -- ἐάν τε
τὸ αὐτὸ πᾶσι γένηται ἐάν τε μὴ τὸ αὐτό

What is attested as being mutually beneficial in the uses of partnership with
one another (among the things thought to be just) does have the character
of what is just — whether it comes to be the same thing for all or not the
same thing

e-an de nó-mon thé-taí Tis + mē A-po-bai-né de ka-ta to sym-phé-ron tés pros al-lé-lous

koi-no-ní-as: ou-ké-ti toü-to ten tod dí-kai-ou phy-sin é-chei

71

ἐὰν δὲ νόμον θῆταί Τις - μὴ Ἀποβαίνῃ δὲ κατὰ τὸ συμφέρον τῆς πρὸς ἀλλήλους

κοινωνίας: οὐκέτι τοῦτο τὴν τοῦ δίκαιου φύσιν ἔχει

then if Someone sets up a law - but it does have a result in accordance with

what mutually benefits in partnership with one another: this no longer has
the nature of what is just

kan me-ta-pip-té to ka-ta to di-kai-on Sym-phé-ron — chró-non dé ti-na eis ten pró-lep-sin

e-nar-mot-té: ou-den ét-ton — e-kei-non ton chró-non — én dí-kai-on tois me phd-nais

ke-nais he-au-tous syn-ta-rát-tou-sin (al-l'eis ta prág-ma-ta blé-pou-sin)

κἂν μεταπίπτῃ τὸ κατὰ τὸ δίκαιον Συμφέρον -: χρόνον δέ τινα εἰς τὴν πρόληψιν

ἐναρμόττῃ: οὐδὲν ἧττον -- ἐκεῖνον τὸν χρόνον -- nv δίκαιον τοῖς μὴ φωναῖς

κεναῖς ἑαυτοὺς συνταράττουσιν (ἀλλ’ εἰς τὰ πράγματα βλέπουσιν)

even if that which mutually benefits in regards to what is just shifts -- yet for

a certain time it fits into the anticipation: then nevertheless — for that time —

it is just to those not confusing themselves with empty statements (but to

those looking into the situation)

If a law or action brings mutual benefit: then it is just, regardless of uniformity - if it

does not bring mutual benefit, it is not just.

No ordinance or decree makes a law just, but only the fact that it results in a

practical and mutual benefit.

Even if circumstances change so that what was once advantageous according to

justice is no longer so — it was still just when it was advantageous.

KA 38. Contextual Justice

én-tha — mé kai nón ge-no-mé-nén tôn pe-ri-es-tó-tón prag-ma-t6n — a-ne-fé-né mē

har-mót-ton-ta eis ten pró-lep-sin ta No-mis-thén-ta dí-kai-a e-p'au-tón tôn ér-gón: ouk

én tad-ta di-kai-a

ἔνθα — μὴ καινῶν γενομένων τῶν περιεστώτων πραγμάτων — ἀνεφάνη μὴ
ἁρμόττοντα εἰς τὴν πρόληψιν τὰ Νομισθέντα δίκαια ἐπ’ αὐτῶν τῶν ἔργων: οὐκ
ἦν ταῦτα δίκαια

whenever — without the surrounding situations becoming new — the things
thought to be just in the tasks themselves display that they not to fit with
the anticipation: then it is not possible that they are just

én-tha de — kai-nón ge-no-mé-non tón prag-má-ton — ou-ké-ti sy-né-phe-re ta au-ta di-kai-a

Ke(-me-na:

ἔνθα δὲ — καινῶν γενομένων τῶν πραγμάτων — οὐκέτι συνέφερε τὰ αὐτὰ δίκαια
Κείμενα:

but whenever — with the situations becoming new -- the same things set up
as just are no longer mutually beneficial:

en-taü-tha, dē tó-te men ên di-kai-a (hó-te sy-né-phe-ren eis ten pros al-lé-lous

koi-no-ní-an tôn sym-po-li-teu-o-mé-non) - hy-ste-ron d’ouk én é-ti dí-kai-a (hó-te me

Sy-né-phe-ren)

ἐνταῦθα, δὴ τότε μὲν ἦν δίκαια (ὅτε συνέφερεν εἰς τὴν πρὸς ἀλλήλους
κοινωνίαν τῶν συμπολιτευομένων) - ὕστερον δ’, οὐκ ἦν ἔτι δίκαια (ὅτε μὴ
συνέφερεν)

in this respect, indeed while at that time they are just (when they are
mutually beneficial in the partnership of fellow-citizens with one another) --
but afterwards, it is not possible that they are still just (when they are not
mutually beneficial)

If what was once considered just does not fit with our anticipation of justice — then
what was once considered just was not truly just.

When circumstances do change — actions that were once beneficial and just can
become unjust if they no longer provide mutual benefit.

He who best composes himself against external threats makes what he can of the
same type as himself = but what he cannot make of the same type, he does not treat
as alien -- yet from what is not even possible to not treat as alien, he does keep
unmixed, removing whatever is beneficial to remove.

KA 40. The Complete Life

Hó-soi tén dy-na-min Es-chon tod to thar-rein má-lis-ta ek tôn ho-mor-rou-ton

pa-ra-skeu-á-sa-sthai (hoü-to Καὶ e-bi-d-san me-t'al-lé-lon hé-dis-ta) to be-bai-d-ta-ton

pís-to-ma é-chon-tes

Ὅσοι τὴν δύναμιν ἔσχον τοῦ τὸ θαρρεῖν μάλιστα ἐκ τῶν ὁμορρούντων
παρασκευάσασθαι (οὕτω καὶ ἐβίωσαν μετ’ αλλήλων ἥδιστα) τὸ βεβαιότατον

πίστωµα ἔχοντες

As many people as have the force of providing for themselves the greatest
security from those bordering (and in this way also live their life among one
another most pleasantly) have the most affirmed assurance

kai (ple-res-tá-tén oij-kei-o-té-ta a-po-la-bón-tes) ouk 6-dy-ran-to, hds pros é-le-on, ten

toü te-leu-té-san-tos pro-ka-tas-tro-phén

καὶ (πληρεστάτην οἰκειότητα ἀπολαβόντες) οὐκ ὠδύραντο, ὡς πρὸς ἔλεον, τὴν
τοῦ τελευτήσαντος προκαταστροφήν

and (having fully acquired the fullest compatibility) they do not mourn, as
though with pity, the prior demise of he who died

77

Those who are able to create and maintain secure and pleasant relationships with
others live more contentedly in the reassurance and comfort of these bonds.

Yet such people also do not experience deep mourning or regret when a friend
passes away — because the strength and joy of their relationship provides continued
pleasure that outweighs the sorrow of loss.

VATICAN SAYINGS

Sententiae Vaticanae

Also called Gnomologium Vaticanum Epicureum and Ἐπικούρου Γνωμολογία and
Ἐπικούρου Προσφώνησεις.

"The Vatican Sayings" is a collection of 81 short sayings — mainly from Epicurus but
also from later Epicureans, likely compiled between the 3 and 5 centuries AD.

It survives in a 14th-century manuscript in the Vatican Library. (vaticanus Graecus 1950)

The Vatican Sayings that have an equivalent in the Κύριαι Δόξαι (Essential
Judgments) are not included here.

ἔχει τὸν χρόνον - ἡ δὲ χρονίζουσα περὶ τὴν σάρκα ἀβληχρὸν ἔχει τὸν πόνον

VS 7. Folly of Concealment

A-di-koün-ta, la-thein men dy-sko-lon -- pís-tin de la-bein hy-per τοῦ la-thein a-dy-na-ton

Ἀδικοῦντα, λαθεῖν μὲν δύσκολον - πίστιν δὲ λαβεῖν ὑπὲρ τοῦ λαθεῖν ἀδύνατον

for one being unjust, to evade notice is discontenting -- but to gain

confidence about evading notice is impossible

This is a summary of KA 35 "It is not possible for one who evasively brings about
something (among those things that they mutually agreed with one another in not
harming and not being harmed) to believe that he will evade notice — even if he

evades thousands of times up to the present — for until his overturning, it is unclear if

he will still evade notice."

Attributed to Metrodorus.

Mé-mné-so hó-ti (thn&-tós hón tê phy-sei, kai la-bón chró-non hó-ris-mé-non) A-né-bés tois

pe-ri phy-se-os di-a-lo-gis-mois — he-pi ten a-pei-ri-an kai ton ai-d-na — kai Ka-tei-des:

"tá t'e-On-ta, tá t'es-só-me-na, pró t'e-ón-ta"

Μέμνησο ὅτι (θνητός ὤν τῇ Φύσει, καὶ λαβών χρόνον ὡρισμένον) Ἀνέβης τοῖς
περὶ φύσεως διαλογισμοῖς -- ἐπὶ τὴν ἀπειρίαν καὶ τὸν αἰῶνα -- καὶ Κατεῖδες:
"τά τ᾽ ἐόντα, TAT ἐσσόμενα, πρό τ᾽ ἐόντα"

Recall that (while being mortal by nature, and having finite time) you
ascended with thorough reasonings about nature — regarding infinity and
eternity — and have looked upon: "the things existing, the things that will
exist, and the things existing before"

"declaring the things that exist, the things that will exist, and the things existing

before (εἰρεῦσαι τά τ᾽ ἐόντα TAT ἐσσόμενα πρό τ᾽ ἐόντα)' is a quote of line 38 of

with most people: what is tranquil becomes numb - but what moves itself

by which they slaughter [us] in advance, those who say these things:

καὶ γὰρ τὸ παρὸν ὡς μικρὸν (μᾶλλον δὲ μηδ᾽ ὁτιοῦν) πρὸς τὰ σύμπαν

καὶ πρὸς οὐθὲν ἀξιόλογον γεγονότων

85

for they too, having dishonored the present as small (or rather, as nothing at
all) in comparison to the whole: they let it go unenjoyed, and they make light
of virtue and action — as if disheartened and scorning themselves, as
ephemeral and unstable and born for nothing worthy

Alternative Version 2

Stobaeus (n; 450“), Florilegium 16.28

Ἐπικούρου "Γεγόναμεν ἅπαξ - δὶς οὐκ ἔστι γενέσθαι. Aci δὲ, τὸν αἰνῶνα, μκέτ'
εἶναι. Σὺ, δὲ, τὴς αὔριον οὐκ Öv Κύριος, ἀναβάλλῃ τὸν καιρόν! ὁ δὲ πάντων Βίος
μελλησμῷ παραπόλλυται - καὶ διὰ τοῦτο, Ἕκαστος ἡμῶν, ἀσχολούμενος,
ἀποθνήσκει"

Of Epicurus "We have been produced once - but twice it is not possible to
be produced: it is necessary, for eternity, to no longer exist. You, however,
not being tomorrow's Master, postpone cheer! The life of all is frittered
away by procrastination — and because of this, each of us, while being
occupied, passes away"

Harvest the Day

Horatius, Carmina 1.11

Tū - ne quaesieris — scire néfas! quem mihi, quem tíbi

You — do not seek — it is not to be found! What to me, what to you

finem Di déderint, Λευκονοή. Nec Babylóniós

the Gods will give as an end, Leuconoé. Nor with Babylonian

temptaris nuimerds. ut mélius (quídquid érit) pat,

numbers should you tamper. How much better (whatever will be) to endure,

seu plüris Hiemés seu tríbuit láppiter áltimam,

whether more winters Jupiter grants or the last,

(quae nunc oppósitis debílitat pümícibus máre

(which now with opposing pumice disables the Sea

Tyrrhénum). Sápias: vína liqués et spátio brévi

of Tyrrhenia). Be wise: you should filter the wine and in short time

spem lóngam réseces! dum lóquimur, fügerit ínvida

you should cut back long hope! While we speak, in envy will have fled

ætās: carpe diem quam minimum crédula póstero

the age: harvest the day which expects the least in tomorrow

VS 47. Defiance of Chance

Attributed to Metrodorus.

Προκατείλημμαί σε — ὦ Τύχη! — καὶ πᾶσαν σὴν παρείσδυσιν ἐνέφραξα

| have thoroughly anticipated you — O Chance! — and | obstructed all your
infiltration

kai oU-te soi oU-te al-lé ou-de-mí-à pe-ri-stá-sei Dó-so-men he-au-tous ek-dó-tous — all

hó-tan hé-mas to Chre-on e-xá-ge:

καὶ οὔτε σοὶ οὔτε ἀλλῇ οὐδεμίᾳ περιστάσει Δώσομεν ἑαυτοὺς ἐκδότους ~ ἀλλ'
ὅταν ἡμᾶς τὸ Χρεὼν ἐξάγη:

and neither to you nor to any other circumstance will we give ourselves as
given up ~ but whenever what must happen leads us out:

mé-ga pros-ptü-san-tes tô zén kai tois au-tó ke-nós pe-ri-plat-to-mé-nois — Á-pi-men ek

τοῦ zén me-ta ka-loü pai-Ó-nos, e-pi-pho-noün-tes hos [εὖ] hé-min be-bí-o-tai!

τοῦ ζῆν μετὰ καλοῦ παιῶνος, ἐπιφωνοῦντες ὡς [εὐ] ἡμῖν βεβίωται!

having greatly spat on living and on those who are enwrapped in it in vain --
we go out of living with a correct song, exclaiming that it has been lived
[well] for us!

VS 63. Balance

és-ti kai en lep-tó-te-ti ka-tha-ri-ó-tes — hés ho A-ne-pi-lÓ-gis-tos, pa-ra-plé-si-ón ti pás-chei

tô di'-a-o-ris-ti-an ek-píp-ton-ti

ἔστι καὶ ἐν λεπτότητι καθαριότης — ἧς ὁ Ἀνεπιλόγιστος, παραπλήσιόν τι πάσχει
τῷ δι' ἀοριστίαν ἐκπίπτοντι

there is indeed a clarity in subtlety [/.e., in simple living] - he who does not
fully inductively consider this, experiences something nearly equivalent to he
who falls out through indefiniteness [i.e., to one who falls into the

limitlessness of extravagant living]

[Long and Sedley] There can be refinement even on slender means, and one who
fails to take account of it is in a similar position to someone who goes astray

through ignoring limits.

[Peter Saint-Andre] There is an elegance in simplicity, and one who is thoughtless

resembles one whose feelings run to excess.

The manuscript's reading (which appears to be "ἐν λεπτότητι καθάριος") is
somewhat difficult — which has led many editors to make changes. Usener changed
"ἐν λεπτότητι καθαριότης" (a purity in subtlety) to

"ἐν λιτότητι µεθόριος" (a boundary in simplicity).

122

In The Double Indictment Lucian has Zeus complain "I myself have to do any number
of tasks that are almost impossible to carry out on account of their subtlety (ὑπὸ

λεπτότητος).' (Double Indictment, section 2)

[Bailey] Frugality too has a limit, and the man who disregards it is in like case
with him who errs through excess.

[Inwood and Gerson] There is also a proper measure for parsimony, and he who
does not reason it out is as badly off as he who goes wrong by total neglect

of limit.
